⚫

Electricity and Magnetism by T. Matsushita

⚫

Basic Network Theory by J. Vlach

⚫

Integrated Electronics by J. Millman and C. Holkias

a)

Course Overview and description

Basic Electrical Engineering II builds upon the foundational concepts introduced in Basic
Electrical Engineering I, extending students’ understanding from classical circuit theory into the
domain of semiconductor physics and electronic devices. The course begins with an introduction
to the atomic structure and the fundamental principles governing semiconductors, including the
behavior of charge carriers, effective mass, mobility, conductivity, carrier lifetime, and
recombination phenomena. Students are then introduced to transport mechanisms such as drift,
diffusion, continuity equations, and the Hall effect, which provide the basis for analyzing
semiconductor behavior in practical applications. The course progresses to the study of PN
junction materials, diodes, and their practical uses in rectification, filtering, and voltage
regulation circuits, with emphasis on Zener diode applications. Building on this, transistor theory
is explored in depth, covering bipolar junction transistors (BJT) in common-base, commonemitter, and common-collector configurations, as well as field-effect transistors (FETs). Their
static and dynamic characteristics are analyzed, and their role as switching devices and amplifiers
is emphasized. Students will also engage with small-signal amplifier design and AC/DC load line
analysis to strengthen both analytical and design skills. By the end of the course, students are
expected to not only understand the theoretical principles of semiconductor devices but also
apply them in designing, analyzing, and interpreting basic electronic circuits, thereby bridging
the gap between circuit theory and modern electronics. This prepares students for advanced
133

courses in electronics and equips them with problem-solving and design competencies relevant
to real-world electrical engineering applications.
b) Pre-requisites: Nil
c) Co-requisite(s): Nil
d) Role in Curriculum: Core

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Basic Electrical Engineering II is highly relevant in today’s technology-driven world, where
semiconductors and electronic devices form the backbone of modern industries. From consumer

140

electronics and renewable energy systems to telecommunications and automation, the principles
of semiconductor physics, PN junctions, transistors, and amplifiers are central to innovation.
With the rapid growth of microelectronics, VLSI, and nanotechnology, engineers require strong
foundational knowledge to address issues of miniaturization, efficiency, and sustainability. This
course equips students with the skills to contribute to advancements in electronics, power
systems, and emerging fields like IoT and smart devices, ensuring industrial and societal impact.

Course Overview and description

This course covers the fundamentals of electromechanical devices and transformers, including
energy conversion principles, transformer performance and types, DC machine operation,
excitation, characteristics, and applications. It emphasizes practical aspects such as
motor/generator selection, speed control, and braking, preparing students to analyze and apply
machines and transformers in real engineering systems.

Group works and Assignments
Coursework requires students to analyze the behavior of transformers and DC machines,

differentiating their types and examining their characteristics. Through collaborative and
individual assignments, students apply principles of electromechanical energy conversion to
evaluate performance and select appropriate machines for practical applications, thereby
solidifying their ability to describe and explain fundamental operations.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
The course equips students to analyze the performance of transformers and DC machines

and evaluate their suitability for real-world applications, emphasizing reliable, efficient, and safe
operation. Through the application of design and selection principles, students learn to propose
robust solutions that meet operational constraints, supporting sustainable engineering practices.

Course Code: EEE 315

Course Title: Electromagnetic Fields and Waves I

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Three hours lectures per week
Location: Hall C3-6

Lecturer(s): Engr. Dr. A. O. Ojo

Textbooks and other Materials:
- Owolabi & Ojo (2022), Electromagnetic Fields and Waves – Principles and Applications
Engineering Electromagnetics, 1st edition, Matprints Inc.
- Gunther Lehner (2010), Electromagnetic Field Theory for Engineers and Physicists, Springer
Heidelberg Dordrecht London New York
a)

Course Overview and description

This course covers the fundamental concepts of electromagnetics, emphasizing the mathematical
tools and physical principles essential for analyzing and solving problems in electrical
engineering. Topics include vector analysis, integral theorems, and the behavior of electric and
magnetic fields governed by laws such as Coulomb’s Law, Gauss’s Law, Faraday’s Law, BiotSavart and Ampere’s Law. The course covers electric fields and electric potentials for various
charge distributions, including lines, rings, surfaces, volumes, and electric dipoles. A key focus is
on Maxwell’s Equations and their role in understanding electromagnetic wave propagation in
various media, as well as practical applications such as wave behavior, reflection, refraction, and
transmission, and modern communication systems and their societal and ethical impacts.
b)

Course Code: EEE 319

Course Title: Measurement and Instrumentation

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Engineering Auditorium Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M.O Onibonoje, Mr. Victor
Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
⚫

Measurement and Instrumentation Principle by Alan S. Morris

⚫

Electrical Technology by B. L. Theraja and A.K. Theraja

⚫

A Course in Electronic and Electrical Measurement & Instrumentation by Gupta

⚫

Basic Concepts of Medical Instrumentation by Walter H. Olson

a)

Course Overview and description

Measurement and Instrumentation introduces students to the fundamental principles,
techniques, and tools essential for accurate measurement in electrical and electronic engineering.
The course provides a solid grounding in units, symbols, and standards of measurement,
followed by a study of the theory of errors, distinguishing between systematic and random errors
and methods for minimizing their impact on results. Students will explore a wide range of
instruments, beginning with traditional indicating devices such as moving coil, moving iron,
dynamometer, and electrostatic instruments, before progressing to advanced measuring systems
154

for DC and AC quantities, including impedance, power, and energy measurements. The use of
instrument potentiometers, oscilloscopes, and cathode-ray tubes (CRT) for measurement
applications forms a crucial part of the curriculum, along with the study of pen recorders and
modern digital test instruments. In addition, the course introduces students to practical issues
such as signal interference, screening, and grounding techniques that ensure accuracy and
reliability in measurement systems. Through this comprehensive coverage, students develop the
ability to identify and understand key electrical quantities, interpret measurement results, and
effectively apply modern test equipment in experimental and industrial contexts. The course
outcomes emphasize both conceptual understanding and practical application, aligning with
broader programme learning objectives that encourage problem-solving, communication, ethical
responsibility, and the integration of technology in engineering practice. Ultimately, EEE 319
equips students with the essential skills to analyze, interpret, and communicate measurement
data, preparing them for advanced studies and professional practice in electrical and electronic
engineering.

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Measurement and Instrumentation is highly relevant to contemporary engineering practice, as
160

accurate measurement underpins technological innovation, industrial automation, and quality
assurance. In modern industries such as power systems, telecommunications, and electronics
manufacturing, reliable instrumentation ensures efficiency, safety, and compliance with
international standards. With the growing integration of digital test instruments, smart sensors,
and real-time monitoring systems, engineers must adapt to rapidly evolving technologies. This
course equips students with the skills to address these industrial needs, fostering competence in
error analysis, data interpretation, and advanced measurement tools essential for tackling present
and future engineering challenges.

Course Code: EEE 324

Course Title: Linear systems

Semester: Second

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B2-5

Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M. O. Onibonoje, Mr. Victor

Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
◼

Linear System by H. Bourles

◼

Control System by Norman Nise

◼

Feedback and Control Systems by Joseph J. DiStefano, Allen R. Stubberud and Ivan J.
Williams

a)

Course Overview and description

Linear Systems provides students with a comprehensive foundation in the modeling, analysis,
and evaluation of dynamic systems across electrical, mechanical, and thermal domains. The
course begins by introducing mathematical models of physical systems and develops the concept
of system analogies, enabling learners to translate problems from one physical domain to another.
Emphasis is placed on control system classifications and feedback concepts, highlighting their

161

role in shaping system performance and ensuring desired outputs under varying conditions.
Students explore transfer functions, block diagrams, and signal flow graphs, building the skills
to represent, simplify, and interconnect systems efficiently. The course further investigates
system responses, covering both transient and steady-state behaviors, and equips students with
frequency-domain analysis tools such as root-locus, Bode plots, Nyquist diagrams, and polar
plots for performance characterization and design insights. Stability analysis forms a crucial
component, with techniques such as Routh-Hurwitz, Nyquist, and Bode criteria applied to
determine robustness and reliability of systems. Finally, an introduction to analogue computer
simulation enhances practical understanding by demonstrating system behavior and control
strategies. By the end of the course, students will be able to model real-world systems, analyze
responses, assess stability, and apply feedback principles, thereby gaining a strong analytical base
for advanced courses in control engineering, signal processing, and system design. The
knowledge gained equips learners to tackle contemporary industrial challenges where system
modeling and control are vital, from automation and robotics to energy systems and
communication networks.
b) Pre-requisites: Nil
c) Co-requisite(s): Nil
d) Role in Curriculum: Core

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

167

Linear systems form the backbone of modern engineering applications, from automation and
robotics to power and communication networks. With industries increasingly reliant on control,
modeling, and stability analysis for reliable and efficient operations, knowledge of system
dynamics is crucial. This course equips students with tools to analyze performance, ensure
stability, and optimize feedback in real-world systems. Contemporary challenges such as
renewable energy integration, smart grids, autonomous vehicles, and advanced manufacturing
highlight the industrial relevance of linear systems, making the course essential for addressing
emerging technological and sustainability demands.

Course Code: EEE 321

Course Title: Electric Circuit Theory I

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Three hours lectures per week
Location: Hall C3-6

Lecturer(s): Engr. Dr. A. O. Ojo & Engr O. Imahe

Textbooks and other Materials:
- Charles Alexander & Matthew Sadiku (2021), Circuit-Fundamentals of Electric Circuits 7th
Ed (1).
a)

Course Overview and description

Circuit elements, circuit theorems, applications. Network response to steps, ramp, impulses.
Network functions, response to exponential, sinusoidal sources. Laplace transform, polezero
analysis, network synthesis, resonance, two-point analysis, ladder network.
transformation, T, P Networks.
b)

Pre-requisites: Nil

c)

Co-requisite(s): Nil

12)

Course Content or Outline

171

Topics

Weeks

Fundamentals of Circuit Elements and Theorems
• Passive and active circuit elements: resistors, capacitors, inductors,
sources
• Circuit laws: Ohm’s Law, Kirchhoff’s Voltage and Current Laws
• Thevenin and Norton equivalents, Superposition and Maximum
Power Transfer Theorems
• Applications of circuit theorems in DC and AC analysis
Network Response to Excitations
• Transient and steady-state analysis of circuits
• Response to step, ramp, and impulse inputs
• Time-domain behavior of RL, RC, and RLC circuits
• Initial and final conditions, natural and forced responses
Network Functions and Sinusoidal Analysis
• Introduction to network functions and transfer functions
• Frequency response and Bode plots
• Response to exponential and sinusoidal sources
• Phasor analysis and complex impedance
Laplace Transform and Network Synthesis
• Laplace transform fundamentals and inverse techniques
• Application of Laplace transform in circuit analysis
• Pole-zero analysis and system stability
• Resonance in electrical networks
• Ladder networks and two-point configurations
Advanced Network Configurations and Transformations
• Star-Delta transformation and its applications
• T and Pi network configurations
• Optimization of circuit performance using transformations
• Analysis of complex network topologies.

Tutorials

172

l)

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments in Circuit Theory I are designed to challenge students

to think critically and solve problems within the domain of electrical circuits, encouraging
analysis of real-world scenarios and the application of circuit principles to propose effective
solutions. These tasks also reinforce students’ understanding through calculations, analytical
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving modern circuit configurations
and network behaviors.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Electrical circuit theory faces modern challenges such as miniaturization, energy

efficiency, and integration with smart technologies like IoT and intelligent control systems.
Engineers must also address cybersecurity in embedded systems and rely on advanced
simulation tools to model increasingly complex circuit behaviors. This course is vital across
industries including power, telecommunications, consumer electronics, and automotive, where
circuit design directly impacts system performance and reliability. From optimizing energy
distribution to enabling smart devices and automation, circuit theory remains a cornerstone of
innovation and engineering practice.

13)

Course Content or Outline

Topics

Weeks

Advanced Network Analysis Techniques
• Node and loop analysis methods for complex circuits
• Matrix formulation and solution of network equations
• Application of network theorems in multi-loop systems
Network Functions and Transfer Function Realization
• Definition and properties of network functions
• Realization of transfer functions using passive and active
components
• Foster and Cauer forms of network synthesis
Filter Design and Analysis
• Classification and characteristics of filters: low-pass, high-pass,
band-pass
• Butterworth and Chebyshev filter design principles
• Frequency response and bandwidth considerations
Non-Linear Circuit Analysis and Locus Diagrams
• Behavior of non-linear components in circuits
• Graphical analysis using locus diagrams
• Stability and response of non-linear networks
Active Network Synthesis and Applications
• Design of active networks using operational amplifiers
• Realization of complex transfer functions
• Practical considerations in active filter implementation
Advanced Filter Structures and Optimization
• M-derived and constant-k filters
• Filter cascading and impedance matching
• Design for minimal distortion and optimal power handling

k)

Tutorials
Tutorial will be offered at the end of each module.

l)

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments in Circuit Theory II are designed to challenge students

to think critically and solve complex problems in electrical network analysis and synthesis.
Students will engage with real-world circuit scenarios, applying advanced concepts such as
transfer function realization, filter design, and non-linear analysis to propose effective
engineering solutions. The course also includes assignments aimed at reinforcing understanding
through rigorous calculations, analytical exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments
involving modern circuit configurations and active network synthesis.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Circuit Theory II addresses modern challenges such as the need for high-performance

filter design, efficient network synthesis, and the analysis of non-linear systems in increasingly
complex electrical environments. It also engages with emerging demands in signal integrity,
active circuit optimization, and the integration of analog-digital hybrid systems in smart
technologies. This course is highly relevant to industries such as power electronics, embedded
systems, telecommunications hardware, and control engineering, where precise circuit behavior
and network design are critical. It supports technologies like active filter implementation, transfer
function realization, and operational amplifier-based systems, all essential for developing
reliable, scalable, and efficient electronic solutions in modern industrial applications.

a)

Course Overview and description

This course explores the production of rotating magnetic fields in AC machines. Simple theory of
three-phase induction motors; torque speed characteristics, Single-phase induction motorsapplications. Selection of motors, for practical applications. Synchronous machines.

Course Content or Outline

182

Topics

Weeks

Introduction to AC Machines

1 -2

•

Overview of electric machines (DC vs AC).

•

Role of AC machines in power systems and industry.

•

Basic review of magnetic circuits and electromagnetic induction.

•

Principle of rotating magnetic field (RMF).

•

Mathematical representation of RMF in three-phase systems.

•

Phasor diagrams and RMF speed relation

Introduction to Three-Phase Induction Motors:
•

Constructional features (stator, rotor: squirrel cage & wound type).

•

Principle of operation: induction principle.

•

Concept of slip and rotor frequency.

•

Equivalent Circuit of Induction Motors.

•

Power flow diagram of induction motors.

•

Torque-Speed Characteristics of Induction Motors

•

Speed control: stator voltage control, rotor resistance control, pole

3-6

changing, frequency control.
•

Three-phase induction motor tests

•

Industrial and commercial applications.

•

Selection criteria: torque, speed, power rating.

Single-Phase Induction Motors – Principles
•

Need for single-phase motors.

•

Double revolving field theory.

•

Types: split-phase, capacitor-start, capacitor-run, shaded pole

7-8

motors.
•

Applications of Single-Phase Induction Motors

•

Performance comparison with 3-phase induction motors.

Synchronous Machines – (Alternators and Motors)
•

Construction of synchronous machines.

•

Principle of operation: synchronous speed, excitation.

•

Types: cylindrical rotor, salient pole.

•

Equivalent circuit of synchronous machines (Alternators and

10 - 12

Motors)
•

Applications of Synchronous Machines

•

Comparing AC machine types (induction vs synchronous vs singlephase).

13 - 15

Assignments
An assignment focused on production of rotating magnetic field in three phase machine
analyzing slip, speed and torque production.

m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

The course equips students with the knowledge to address critical challenges in AC machine
selection for application. By understanding the construction, working principle and operation of
these machines, students can contribute to the selection and installation for practical industry
application.

SECTION FIVE: CURRICULUM AND LEARNING
PROCESS

185

The Curriculum
The Curriculum
Discuss the program structure and course contents to show how they are appropriate to,
consistent with, and support the development of the range of intellectual and practical skills
and attainment or achievement of the PLOs:
The program structure and course contents are designed to be appropriate, consistent, and
supportive of developing a range of intellectual and practical skills, ensuring the attainment of
the Programme Learning Outcomes (PLOs). The curriculum is carefully crafted to provide a
comprehensive education in Computer Engineering, integrating both theoretical knowledge and
hands-on experience. Each course is aligned with the PLOs, contributing to the overall
educational objectives of the programme. Core courses cover fundamental concepts, advanced
theories, and practical applications, ensuring that students gain a deep understanding of the field.
Elective courses allow students to specialize in areas of interest, further enhancing their expertise
and skills. Laboratory work, projects, and internships are integral components of the programme,
providing students with opportunities to apply their knowledge in real-world scenarios. These
practical experiences are essential for developing problem-solving abilities, technical proficiency,
and professional competencies. The curriculum includes courses on ethics, communication, and
management to ensure that graduates are well-rounded professionals capable of addressing
complex challenges in their careers. The programme structure and course contents are
meticulously designed to support the holistic development of students, equipping them with the
necessary skills and knowledge to achieve the PLOs and excel in the field of Computer
Engineering.
Distribution of the Engineering Courses
Visit departmental handbook for details on the course structure and course contents at
departmental website

197

i. Compulsory Courses: Students must register for and pass before graduation.
ii. Elective Courses: Courses students may offer and pass to obtain the minimum units required
for graduation. Such courses are normally selected from within or outside the programme with
the approval of the department.
iii. Required Course: A course specified by a programme which a student shall take and pass.

187

iv. Pre-requisite Courses: Courses students must take and pass before taking another specified
course.
v. Institutional Courses: Courses whose knowledge is essential for the preparation of Afe
Babalola University for future employment after graduation.
vi. Carry-over Courses: On the account of any failure by a student to satisfy a pass requirement
in any course, such student will be required to repeat only the course/courses the following
academic session or re-write the prescribed examination in such course at the end of the academic
session.
Students carrying over courses shall not be allowed to register for more than the approved
maximum number of credits for the semester.

COURSE DESCRIPTION
100 LEVEL
GST 111: Communication in English (2 Units C: LH=15; PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, students should be able to:
1. identify possible sound patterns in English Language;
2. list notable language skills;
3. classify word formation processes;
4. construct simple and fairly complex sentences in English;
5. apply logical and critical reasoning skills for meaningful presentations;
6. demonstrate an appreciable level of the art of public speaking and listening; and
7. write simple and technical reports.
Course Contents
Sounds and sound patterns in English Language (vowels and consonants, phonetics and
phonology). English word classes (lexical and grammatical words, definitions, forms, functions,
usages, collocations). major word formation processes; the sentence in English (types: structural
and functional). grammar and usage (tense, concord and modality). Reading and types of
reading, comprehension skills, 3RsQ. Logical and critical thinking; reasoning methods (logic and
syllogism, inductive and deductive argument, analogy, generalisation and explanations). Ethical
considerations, copyright rules and infringements. Writing activities: pre-writing (brainstorming
and outlining). writing (paragraphing, punctuation and expression). post- writing (editing and
proofreading). Types of writing (summary, essays, letter, curriculum vitae, report writing, notemaking) etc. Mechanics of writing. Information and Communication Technology in modern
language learning. Language skills for effective communication. The art of public speaking.
GET 101: Engineer in Society (1 Unit C: LH=15)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. differentiate between science, engineering and technology, and relate them to innovation;

194

2. distinguish between the different cadres of engineering – engineers, technologists, technicians
and craftsmen and their respective roles and competencies;
3. identify and distinguish between the relevant professional bodies in engineering;
4. categorise the goals of global development or sustainable development goals (SDGs); and
5. identify and evaluate safety and risk in engineering practice.
Course Contents
History, evolution and philosophy of science. engineering and technology. The engineering
profession – engineering family (engineers, technologists, technicians and craftsmen),
professional bodies and societies. Engineers' code of conduct and ethics, and engineering literacy.
Sustainable development goals (SDGs), innovation, infrastructures and nation building economy, politics, business. Safety and risk analysis in engineering practice. Engineering
competency skills – curriculum overview, technical, soft and digital skills. Guest seminars and
invited lectures from different engineering professional associations.
MTH 101: Elementary Mathematics I (Algebra and Trigonometry)
(2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course students should be able to:
1. define and explain set, subset, union, intersection, complements, and demonstrate the use of
Venn diagrams;
2. solve quadratic equations;
3. solve trigonometric functions;
4. identify various types of numbers; and
5. solve some problems using binomial theorem.
Course Contents
Elementary set theory, subsets, union, intersection, complements, Venn diagrams. Real numbers,
integers, rational and irrational numbers. Mathematical induction, real sequences and series,
theory of quadratic equations, binomial theorem, complex numbers, algebra of complex numbers,
the argand diagram. De-Moiré’s theorem, nth roots of unity. Circular measure, trigonometric
functions of angles of any magnitude, addition and factor formulae.
PHY 101: General Physics I (Mechanics) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the students should be able to:

195

1. identify and deduce the physical quantities and their units;
2. differentiate between vectors and scalars;
3. describe and evaluate motion of systems on the basis of the fundamental laws of mechanics;
4. apply Newton’s laws to describe and solve simple problems of motion;
5. evaluate work, energy, velocity, momentum, acceleration, and torque of moving or rotating
objects;
6. explain and apply the principles of conservation of energy, linear and angular momentum;
7. describe the laws governing motion under gravity; and
8. explain motion under gravity and quantitatively determine behaviour of objects moving under
gravity.
Course Contents
Space and time; units and dimension, vectors and scalars, differentiation of vectors: displacement,
velocity and acceleration; kinematics; Newton’s laws of motion (inertial frames, impulse, force
and action at a distance, momentum conservation); relative motion; application of Newtonian
mechanics; equations of motion; conservation principles in physics, conservative forces,
conservation of linear momentum, kinetic energy and work, potential energy, system of particles,
centre of mass; rotational motion; torque, vector product, moment, rotation of coordinate axes
and angular momentum. Polar coordinates; conservation of angular momentum; circular motion;
moments of inertia, gyroscopes and precession; gravitation: Newton’s law of gravitation, Kepler’s
laws of planetary motion, gravitational potential energy, escape velocity, satellites motion and
orbits.
PHY 107: General Practical Physics I (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the student should be able to:
1. conduct measurements of some physical quantities;
2. make observations of events, collect and tabulate data;
3. identify and evaluate some common experimental errors;
4. plot and analyse graphs; and
5. draw conclusions from numerical and graphical analysis of data.
Course Contents
This introductory course emphasizes quantitative measurements. Experimental techniques. The
treatment of measurement errors. Graphical analysis. The experiments include studies of meters,
the oscilloscope, mechanical systems, electrical and mechanical resonant systems, light, heat,
viscosity, etc. (covered in PHY 101, 102, 103 and PHY 104). However, emphasis should be placed

196

on the basic physical techniques for observation, measurements, data collection, analysis, and
deduction.

EEE 102: Introduction to Electrical and Electronics Engineering (2 units C: LH=15)
Learning Outcomes
Students will be able to:
1. comprehend the duties and functions of an Electrical and Electronics Engineer (EEE);
2. state the requirements for the profession and career opportunities;
3. state the careers related to EEE; and
4. explain the future of EEE.
Course Contents
History of Electrical Engineering. Evolution of EEE. Duties of EE Engineers. Areas of
specialisation and work environment. Skill requirements (soft and hard). Qualities for EE
Engineers. Careers related to EEE. Typical course modules. Job outlook/opportunities for EE
Engineers. Future of EEE. Professional registration (NSE, COREN, IEEE, IET, etc.). Passive
components (R, L, C, transformers): descriptive features, including values and colour codes, uses
in electrical circuits.DC and AC signal parameters
ABUAD-CHM 101: General Chemistry I (2 Units C: LH=30)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is essential for students at our university as it builds a strong foundation in chemistry,
aligning with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 4 (Quality Education) and 9 (Industry,
Innovation, and Infrastructure). A deep understanding of chemical principles is crucial for
advancing in scientific research, environmental sustainability, and technological innovation. By
mastering the content, students will be equipped to contribute to sustainable industrial practices,
environmental conservation, and scientific advancements, directly impacting global challenges.
This course fosters critical thinking and problem-solving skills, preparing students to be leaders
in science, technology, and sustainability.

197

Overview
This course introduces the fundamental concepts of chemistry, including atoms, molecules,
elements, and compounds. It explores modern electronic theory, electronic configuration, and
periodicity, and examines molecular structures, chemical bonding, and stoichiometry. Students
will also study the kinetic theory of matter, thermochemistry, reaction rates, equilibrium, and
thermodynamics. Additionally, the course covers the properties of gases, acids, bases, redox
reactions, electrochemistry, and radioactivity, providing a comprehensive understanding of
essential chemical principles.
Objectives
1. understand and describe the basic structure of atoms, molecules, elements, and
compounds.
2. apply electronic configuration and periodicity to explain chemical properties.
3. analyze and predict the shapes and structures of molecules and solids.
4. solve chemical equations and apply principles of stoichiometry, thermodynamics, and
kinetics.
5. understand and apply concepts of redox reactions, electrochemistry, and radioactivity.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. define atom, molecules and chemical reactions;
2. discuss the modern electronic theory of atoms;
3. write electronic configurations of elements on the periodic table;
4. rationalise the trends of atomic radii, ionisation energies, electronegativity of the elements,
based on their position in the periodic table;
5. identify and balance oxidation–reduction equation and solve redox titration problems;
6. draw shapes of simple molecules and hybridised orbitals;
7. identify the characteristics of acids, bases and salts, and solve problems based on their
quantitative relationship;
8. apply the principles of equilibrium to aqueous systems using LeChatelier’s principle to predict
the effect of concentration, pressure and temperature changes on equilibrium mixtures;
9. analyse and perform calculations with the thermodynamic functions, enthalpy, entropy and
free energy; and
10. determine rates of reactions and its dependence on concentration, time and temperature.
Course Contents

198

Atoms, molecules, elements and compounds, and chemical reactions. Modern electronic theory
of atoms. Electronic configuration, periodicity and building up of the periodic table.
Hybridisation and shapes of simple molecules. Valence forces; Structure of solids. Chemical
equations and stoichiometry; chemical bonding and intermolecular forces, kinetic theory of
matter. Elementary thermochemistry; rates of reaction, equilibrium and thermodynamics. Acids,
bases and salts. Properties of gases. Redox reactions and introduction to electrochemistry.
Radioactivity.
ABUAD-CHM 107: General Practical Chemistry I (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Senate-approved relevance
This laboratory course is integral to our university's commitment to Quality Education (SDG 4)
and Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12). It provides hands-on experience in
fundamental chemical techniques, reinforcing theoretical knowledge from CHM 101. By
mastering these practical skills, students develop the ability to conduct accurate experiments,
analyze data, and present findings effectively—essential competencies for careers in science,
industry, and research. The course also promotes safe and sustainable laboratory practices,
preparing students to contribute to sustainable industrial processes and environmental
stewardship.
Overview
This laboratory course offers practical experience in key chemical techniques, complementing the
theoretical content of CHM 101. Students will engage in acid-base titrations, qualitative analysis,
redox reactions, and gravimetric analysis. Emphasis is placed on accurate data collection,
analysis, and presentation, fostering essential laboratory skills. The course prepares students to
apply these techniques in scientific research and industry, ensuring a comprehensive
understanding of experimental chemistry.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. perform acid-base titrations with precision and accuracy.
2. conduct qualitative and quantitative analysis of chemical substances.
3. understand and execute redox reactions and gravimetric analysis.
4. analyze and interpret experimental data effectively.
5. present laboratory findings clearly and professionally.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the general laboratory rules and safety procedures;

199

2. collect scientific data and correct carry out chemical experiments;
3. identify the basic glassware and equipment in the laboratory;
4. state the differences between primary and secondary standards;
5. perform redox titration;
6. record observations and measurements in the laboratory notebooks; and
7. analyse the data to arrive at scientific conclusions.
Course Contents
Laboratory experiments designed to reflect topics presented in courses CHM 101 and CHM 102.
These include acid-base titrations, qualitative analysis, redox reactions, gravimetric analysis, data
analysis and presentation.
GST 112: Nigerian Peoples and Cultures (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the completion of the course, the students are expected to be able:
1. analyse the historical foundation of Nigerian cultures and arts in pre-colonial times;
2. identify and list the major linguistic groups in Nigeria;
3. explain the gradual evolution of Nigeria as a political entity;
4. analyse the concepts of trade and economic self-reliance of Nigerian peoples in relation to
national development;
5. enumerate the challenges of the Nigerian state regarding nation building;
6. analyse the role of the Judiciary in upholding fundamental human rights
7. identify the acceptable norms and values of the major ethnic groups in Nigeria; and
8. list possible solutions to identifiable Nigerian environmental, moral and value problems.
Course Contents
Nigerian history, culture and art up to 1800 (Yoruba, Hausa and Igbo peoples and cultures;
peoples and cultures of the minority ethnic groups). Nigeria under colonial rule (advent of
colonial rule in Nigeria; colonial administration of Nigeria). Evolution of Nigeria as a political
unit (amalgamation of Nigeria in 1914; formation of political parties in Nigeria; nationalist
movement and struggle for independence). Nigeria and challenges of nation building (military
intervention in Nigerian politics; Nigerian Civil War). Concepts of trade and economics of selfreliance (indigenous trade and market system; indigenous apprenticeship system among
Nigerian peoples; trade, skill acquisition and self-reliance). Social justice and national
development (definition and classification of law); Judiciary and fundamental rights. Individuals,
norms and values (basic Nigerian norms and values, patterns of citizenship acquisition;
citizenship and civic responsibilities; indigenous languages, usage and development; negative

200

attitudes and conducts [Cultism, kidnapping and other related social vices]). Re-orientation,
moral and national values (The 3Rs – Reconstruction, Rehabilitation and Re-orientation; reorientation strategies: Operation Feed the Nation (OFN), Green Revolution, Austerity Measures,
War Against Indiscipline and Corruption (WAIC), Mass Mobilization for Self-Reliance, Social
Justice and Economic Recovery (MAMSER), National Orientation Agency (NOA). Current sociopolitical and cultural developments in Nigeria.
GET 102: Engineering Graphics and Solid Modelling I (2 Units C: LH=15; PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. have a good grasp of design thinking and be obsessed with the determination to apply such to
solving simple every day and also complex problems;
2. recognize the fundamental concepts of engineering drawing and graphics;
3. show skills to represent the world of engineering objects in actionable solid models, and put
such models in a form where they can be inputs for simulation and analyses;
4. analyse such models for strength and cost.
5. prepare the objects for modern production and manufacturing techniques of additive and
subtractive manufacturing;
6. recognise that engineering is multidisciplinary in the sense that mechanical, electrical and other
parts of physical structures are modelled in context as opposed to the analytical nature of the
courses they take;and
7. analyse and master the basics of mechanical and thermal loads in engineering systems.
Course Contents
Introduction to design thinking and engineering graphics. First and third angle orthogonal
projections. Isometric projections; sectioning, conventional practices, conic sections and
development. Freehand and guided sketching – pictorial and orthographic. Visualisation and
solid modelling in design, prototyping and product-making. User interfaces in concrete terms.
Design, drawing, animation, rendering and simulation work spaces. Sketching of 3D objects.
Viewports and sectioning to shop drawings in orthographic projections and perspectives.
Automated viewports. Sheet metal and surface modelling. Material selection and rendering. This
course will use latest professional design tools such as fusion 360, solid works, solid edge or
equivalent.
MTH 102: Elementary Mathematics II (Calculus) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course, students should be able to:

201

1. identify the types of rules in differentiation and integration;
2. recognise and understand the meaning of function of a real variable, graphs, limits and
continuity;
3. solve some applications of definite integrals in areas and volumes;
4. solve function of a real variable, plot relevant graphs, identify limits and idea of continuity;
5. identify the derivative as limit of rate of change;
6. identify techniques of differentiation and perform extreme curve sketching;
7. identify integration as an inverse of differentiation;
8. identify methods of integration and definite integrals; and
9. perform integration application to areas, volumes.
Course Contents
Functions of a real variable, graphs, limits and idea of continuity. The derivative, as limit of rate
of change. Techniques of differentiation, maxima and minima. Extreme curve sketching,
integration, definite integrals, reduction formulae, application to areas, volumes (including
approximate integration: Trapezium and Simpson's rule).
PHY 102: General Physics II (Behaviour of Matter) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the students should be able to:
1. explain the concepts of heat and temperature and relate the temperature scales;
2. define, derive and apply the fundamental thermodynamic relations to thermal systems;
3. describe and explain the first and second laws of thermodynamics, and the concept of entropy;
4. state the assumptions of the kinetic theory and apply techniques of describing macroscopic
behaviour;
5. deduce the formalism of thermodynamics and apply it to simple systems in thermal
equilibrium; and
6. describe and determine the effect of forces and deformation of materials and surfaces.
Course Contents
Heat and temperature, temperature scales; gas laws; general gas equation; thermal conductivity;
first Law of thermodynamics; heat, work and internal energy, reversibility; thermodynamic
processes; adiabatic, isothermal, isobaric; second law of thermodynamics; heat engines and
entropy, Zero’s law of thermodynamics; kinetic theory of gases; molecular collisions and mean
free path; elasticity; Hooke's law, Young's shear and bulk moduli; hydrostatics; pressure,
buoyancy, Archimedes' principles; Bernoullis equation and incompressible fluid flow; surface
tension; adhesion, cohesion, viscosity, capillarity, drops and bubbles.

202

PHY 108: General Practical Physics II (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the student should be able to:
1. conduct measurements of some physical quantities;
2. make observations of events, collect and tabulate data;
3. identify and evaluate some common experimental errors;
4. plot and analyse graphs;
5. draw conclusions from numerical and graphical analysis of data; and
6. prepare and present practical reports.
Course Contents
This practical course is a continuation of PHY 107 and is intended to be taught during the second
semester of the 100 level to cover the practical aspect of the theoretical courses that have been
covered with emphasis on quantitative measurements, the treatment of measurement errors, and
graphical analysis. However, emphasis should be placed on the basic physical techniques for
observation, measurements, data collection, analysis and deduction.

ABUAD-CHM 102: General Chemistry II (2 Units C: LH=30)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is crucial to our university's mission of advancing Quality Education (SDG 4) and
fostering Innovation (SDG 9). It provides a comprehensive understanding of organic chemistry,
which is fundamental to multiple scientific disciplines, including pharmaceuticals, materials
science, and environmental science. The course highlights the role of organic chemistry in
developing new materials like fullerenes, which are essential for nanotechnology. By mastering
the concepts and techniques in organic chemistry, students are prepared to contribute to
innovative solutions in health, industry, and sustainability, addressing global challenges through
scientific advancement.
Overview
This course offers an in-depth study of organic chemistry, beginning with its historical
development and significance. It covers the electronic theory, structure determination,
nomenclature, and functional groups of organic compounds. Students will explore reaction
mechanisms, kinetics, stereochemistry, and the chemistry of hydrocarbons, alcohols, ethers,
amines, and other key organic compounds. Additionally, the course introduces the chemistry of

203

selected metals and non-metals, including comparative chemistry of group IA, IIA, and IVA
elements, and transition metal chemistry, with a focus on their practical applications in various
industries.
Objectives
1. understand the historical development and significance of organic chemistry.
2. describe the structure, nomenclature, and functional groups of organic compounds.
3. analyze reaction mechanisms, kinetics, and stereochemistry in organic reactions.
4. explore the chemistry of hydrocarbons, alcohols, ethers, amines, and other key organic
compounds.
5. examine the chemistry of selected metals, non-metals, and transition metals, including
their industrial applications.

Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the importance and development of organic chemistry;
2. define fullerenes and its applications;
3. discuss electronic theory;
4. determine the qualitative and quantitative of structures in organic chemistry;
5. state rules guiding nomenclature and functional group classes of organic chemistry;
6. determine the rate of reaction to predict mechanisms of reaction;
7. identify classes of organic functional group with brief description of their chemistry;
8. discuss comparative chemistry of group 1A, IIA and IVA elements; and
9. describe basic properties of transition metals.
Course Contents
Historical survey of the development and importance of organic chemistry; fullerenes as fourth
allotrope of carbon, uses as nanotubules, nanostructures, nanochemistry. Electronic theory in
organic chemistry. Isolation and purification of organic compounds; determination of structures
of organic compounds including qualitative and quantitative analysis in organic chemistry;
nomenclature and functional group classes of organic compounds. Introductory reaction
mechanism and kinetics. Stereochemistry. The chemistry of alkanes, alkenes, alkynes, alcohols,
ethers, amines, alkyl halides, nitriles, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids and derivatives. The
chemistry of selected metals and non-metals. Comparative chemistry of group IA, IIA and IVA
elements. Introduction to transition metal chemistry.

204

ABUAD- CHM 108: General Practical Chemistry II (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Senate-approved relevance
This practical course is integral to our university's focus on Quality Education (SDG 4) and
Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12). It builds on the foundation established in
CHM 107, enhancing students' proficiency in functional group analysis and quantitative analysis
using volumetric methods. These skills are essential for accurate chemical analysis in research
and industry, promoting sustainable practices in chemical production and environmental
monitoring. By mastering these techniques, students are better prepared to contribute to scientific
innovation and responsible resource management, aligning with global sustainability goals.
Overview
This course continues from CHM 107, offering advanced laboratory experiments in functional
group analysis and quantitative analysis through volumetric methods. Students will refine their
skills in identifying functional groups and conducting precise quantitative measurements. The
course emphasizes accurate data collection, analysis, and interpretation, which are critical for
success in research and industrial applications. By the end of the course, students will have a
deeper understanding of practical chemical analysis and its importance in various scientific
fields.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. perform advanced functional group analysis accurately.
2. conduct quantitative analysis using volumetric methods with precision.
3. analyze and interpret experimental data effectively.
4. develop proficiency in laboratory techniques for chemical analysis.
5. apply practical skills to real-world chemical problems and research.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the general laboratory rules and safety procedures;
2. collect scientific data and correctly carry out chemical experiments;
3. identify the basic glassware and equipment in the laboratory;

205

4. identify and carry out preliminary tests which include ignition, boiling point, melting point,
test on known and unknown organic compounds;
5. carry out solubility tests on known and unknown organic compounds;
6. carry out elemental tests on known and unknown compounds; and
7. carry out functional group/confirmatory test on known and unknown compounds which could
be acidic/basic/ neutral organic compounds.
Course Contents
Continuation of CHM 107. Additional laboratory experiments to include functional group
analysis, quantitative analysis using volumetric methods.

200 LEVEL FIRST SEMESTER
ENT 211: Entrepreneurship and Innovation (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, students should be able to:
1. explain the concepts and theories of entrepreneurship, intrapreneurship, opportunity seeking,
new value creation and risk-taking;
2. state the characteristics of an entrepreneur;
3. analyse the importance of micro and small businesses in wealth creation, employment
generation and financial independence;
4. engage in entrepreneurial thinking;
5. identify key elements in innovation;
6. describe the stages in enterprise formation, partnership and networking, including business
planning;
7. describe contemporary entrepreneurial issues in Nigeria, Africa and the rest of the world; and
8. state the basic principles of e-commerce.
The

concept

of

entrepreneurship

(entrepreneurship,

intrapreneurship/corporate

entrepreneurship); theories, rationale and relevance of entrepreneurship (Schumpeterian and
other perspectives, risk-taking, necessity and opportunity-based entrepreneurship, and creative
destruction); characteristics of entrepreneurs (opportunity seeker, risk-taker, natural and
nurtured, problem solver and change agent, innovator and creative thinker); entrepreneurial
thinking (critical thinking, reflective thinking and creative thinking). Innovation (The concept of
innovation, dimensions of innovation, change and innovation, knowledge and innovation).
Enterprise formation, partnership and networking (basics of business plan, forms of business
ownership, business registration and alliance formation, and joint ventures). Contemporary

206

entrepreneurship issues (knowledge, skills and technology, intellectual property, virtual office
and networking). Entrepreneurship in Nigeria (biography of inspirational entrepreneurs, youth
and women entrepreneurship, entrepreneurship support institutions, youth enterprise networks
and environmental and cultural barriers to entrepreneurship). Basic principles of e-commerce.

GET 201: Applied Electricity I (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
Students will be able to:
1. discuss the fundamental concepts of electricity and electrical d.c. circuits;
2. state, explain and apply the basic d.c. circuit theorems;
3. explain the basic a.c. circuit theory and
4. apply to solution of simple circuits.
Course contents
Fundamental concepts: Electric fields, charges, magnetic fields. current, B-H curves Kirchhoff’s
laws, superposition. Thevenin Norton theorems, Reciprocity, RL, RC, RLC circuits. DC, AC
bridges, Resistance, Capacitance, Inductance measurement, Transducers, Single phase circuits,
Complex j - notation, AC circuits, impedance, admittance, and susceptance.

Newton's laws of motion. Kinetic energy and momentum analyse
GET 209: Engineering Mathematics I (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course, the students should be able to:
1. solve qualitative problems based on vector and matrix analyses such as linear independence
and dependence of vectors, rank etc;
2. describe the concepts of limit theory and nth order differential equations and their applications
to physical phenomena;
3. solve the problems of differentiation of functions of two variables and know about the
maximization and minimization of functions of several variables;
4. describe the applications of double and triple integration in finding the area and volume of
engineering solids, and explain the qualitative applications of Gauss, Stoke’s and Green’s
theorem;
5. explain ordinary differential equations and applications, and develop a mathematical model of
linear differential equations, as well as appreciate the necessary and sufficient conditions for total
differential equations; and
6. analyse basic engineering models through partial differential equations such as wave equation,
heat conduction equation, etc., as well as fourier series, initial conditions and its applications to
different engineering processes.
Course Contents
Limits, continuity, differentiation, introduction to linear first order differential equations, partial
and total derivatives, composite functions, matrices and determinants, vector algebra, vector
calculus, directional derivatives.

character molding, etc.

GET 206: Fundamentals of Engineering Thermodynamics (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. describe basic concepts of thermodynamics, quantitative relations of Zeroth, first, second and
third laws;
2. define and explain system (surrounding, closed and open system), control volume and control
mass, extensive and intensive properties;
3. calculate absolute and gage pressure, and absolute temperature, calculate changes in kinetic,
potential, enthalpy and internal energy;
4. evaluate the properties of pure substances i.e. evaluate the state of the pure substances such as
compressed liquid, saturated liquid-vapour mixture and superheated vapour using property
diagrams and tables; arrange the ideal and real gas equations of state,
5. formulate the first law of thermodynamics for a closed system i.e. organize the change in energy
in the closed systems via heat and work transfer;
6. distinguish heat transfer by conduction, convection and radiation, and calculate the amount of
heat energy transferred;
7. calculate the changes in moving boundary work, spring work, electrical work and shaft work
in closed systems;
8. apply the first law of thermodynamics for closed systems and construct conservation of mass
and energy equations;
9. formulate the first law of thermodynamics to the open systems i.e. describe steady-flow open
system, apply the first law of thermodynamics to the nozzles, diffusers, turbines, compressors,
throttling valves, mixing chambers, heat exchangers, pipe and duct flow;
10. construct energy and mass balance for unsteady-flow processes;
11. evaluate thermodynamic applications using second law of thermodynamics;

212

12. calculate thermal efficiency and coefficient of performance for heat engine, refrigerators and
heat pumps; and
13. restate perpetual-motion machines, reversible and irreversible processes.
Course Contents
Basic concepts, definitions and laws (quantitative relations of Zeroth, first, second and third laws
of thermodynamics). Properties of pure substances: the two-property rule (P-V-T behaviour of
pure substances and perfect gases); state diagrams. The principle of corresponding state;
compressibility relations; reduced pressure; reduced volume; temperature; pseudo-critical
constants. The ideal gas: specific heat, polytropic processes. Ideal gas cycles; Carnot;
thermodynamic cycles, turbines, steam and gas, refrigeration. The first law of thermodynamics –
heat and work, applications to open and closed systems. The steady f low energy equation
(Bernoulli’s equation) and application. Second law of thermodynamics, heat cycles and
efficiencies.
GET 210: Engineering Mathematics II (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course, the students should be able to:
1. describe physical systems using ordinary differential equations (ODEs);
2. explain the practical importance of solving ODEs, solution methods, and analytically solve a
wide range of ODEs, including linear constant coefficient types;
3. numerically solve differential equations using MATLAB and other emerging applications; 4.
perform calculus operations on vector-valued functions, including derivatives, integrals,
curvature, displacement, velocity, acceleration, and torsion, as well as on functions of several
variables, including directional derivatives and multiple integrals;
5. solve problems using the fundamental theorem of line integrals, Green's theorem, the
divergence theorem, and Stokes' theorem, and perform operations with complex numbers; 6.
apply the concept and consequences of analyticity and the Cauchy-Riemann equations and of
results on harmonic and entire functions of complex variables, as well as the theory of conformal
mapping to solve problems from various fields of engineering; and
7. evaluate complex contour integrals directly and by the fundamental theorem, apply the Cauchy
integral theorem in its various versions, and the Cauchy integral formula.
Course Contents
Introduction to ordinary differential equations (ODEs); theory, applications, methods of solution;
second order differential equations. Advanced topics in calculus (vectors and vector valued
function, line integral, multiple integral and their applications). Elementary complex analysis

213

including functions of complex variables, limits and continuity. Derivatives, differentiation rules
and differentiation of integrals. Cauchy-Riemann equation, harmonic functions, basic theory of
conformal mapping, transformation and mapping and its applications to engineering problems.
Special functions.

ABUAD-GET 208: Strength of Materials (3 Units C: LH=45)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is integral to our university's commitment to Quality Education (SDG 4) and Industry,
Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9). It provides a deep understanding of the mechanical
behavior of materials under various loading conditions, which is essential for designing safe and

214

efficient structures. Mastery of these concepts is critical for students pursuing careers in civil,
mechanical, and aerospace engineering. The course equips students with the skills needed to
analyze and design structural components, contributing to innovative and sustainable
infrastructure development, essential for addressing global engineering challenges.
Overview
This course explores the principles of equilibrium, stress-strain relationships, and the application
of Hooke's law to various materials. It covers the effects of loading and temperature changes on
stresses and strains, torsion in circular members, and shear forces in beams. The course also
examines bending moments, bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined
loadings, and stress analysis. Students will gain the knowledge necessary to analyze and design
structural components under different conditions.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. understand the principles of equilibrium in structural systems.
2. analyze stress-strain relationships in materials using generalized Hooke's law.
3. evaluate stresses and strains due to different loading conditions and temperature changes.
4. understand and analyze torsion in circular members.
5. calculate shear forces and bending moments in beams under various loadings.
6. analyze bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined loadings.
7. apply stress analysis techniques to structural components.
8. design safe and efficient structural components considering various mechanical factors.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. recognize a structural system that is stable and in equilibrium;
2. determine the stress-strain relation for single and composite members based on Hooke's law;
3. estimate the stresses and strains in single and composite members due to temperature changes;
4. evaluate the distribution of shear forces and bending moments in beams with distributed and
concentrated loads;
5. determine bending stresses and their use in identifying slopes and deflections in beams;
6. use Mohr's circle to evaluate the normal and shear stresses in a multi-dimensional stress system
and transformation of these stresses into strains;
7. evaluate the stresses and strains due to torsion on circular members; and
8. determine the buckling loads of columns under various fixity conditions at the ends.

215

Course Contents
Consideration of equilibrium; composite members, stress-strain relation. Generalized Hooke's
law. Stresses and strains due to loading and temperature changes. Torsion of circular members.
Shear force, bending moments and bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined
loadings. Stress
Minimum Academic Standards
As stated in the CCMAS.

4. acquire competence in technical documentation (log-book) and presentation (report) of their

working experience but not necessarily limited to the student’s major. The students are exposed
to hands-on activities on workshop safety and ethics, maintenance of tools, equipment and
machines, welding, fabrication and foundry equipment, production of simple devices; electrical
circuits, wiring and installation, etc. (8-10 weeks during the long vacation following 200 level).

input/output , etc; assembler directives, hand-assembling, additional 80x86/Pentium instructions.
Modular programming. Interrupt and service routine. Interfacing of assembly language to C .
Intel 80x87 floating point programming. Introduction to MMX and SSE programming. Motorola
680x0 assembly language programming. Extensive practical engineering problems solving in
assembly language using MASM for Intel, and cross-assembler for Motorola.
EEE 423: Electrical/Electronic Laboratory III (0, 0, 3) 1 Unit
Laboratory work involves winding techniques. Computer hardware and software. Maintenance
and repairs, communication. A laboratory work set up to illustrate topics covered in control
theory, communication principles, electronic engineering, computer techniques and electric
power principles.

hydraulic, pneumatic and thermal systems). Review of frequency-response analysis (Polar, Bode,
Nyquist, Nichols). Review of pole stability and Routh-Hurwitz’s stability criterion. System
classification and time response analysis. Nyquist stability criterion, M- and N- circles, inverse
Nyquist Plots. Bode diagrams and determination of transfer function from asymptotic plots.
Relative stability. Introduction to Feedback compensation. The tracking and regulation
objectives. Compensation techniques and controller design: Lag, Lead and Lead-Lag

design; controller design using Bode, Nichols and Root locus methods. Servomechanical systems.
Servo-motors, tachogenerators, error detectors amplifiers, actuators, valves, etc. and their transfer
functions. Servomechanism position and velocity control systems. Voltage regulators.
Introduction to nonlinear systems.

chosen for practical working experience but not necessarily limited to the student’s major (12
weeks during the long vacation following 200 level).

6.2.

Compliance with Admission Requirements
6.2.1

Inter-University Transfer:

a) Regulations:
Candidates from other accredited institutions can enroll in the Programme. The university
rule stipulates that every candidate must complete a minimum of two academic terms at
the institution to receive the university degree. Those possessing a Higher National
Diploma in a related area may qualify for entry into the 300 level. Each candidate is
assessed according to their own qualifications and achievements.
6.3.

Academic Regulations

The student handbook, published by the University and the Programme, is distributed to all
students. The University handbook includes entry requirements, rules and regulations that
govern examination conduct, the grading system, penalties for examination malpractice,
probation, expulsion, withdrawals, graduation, and other essential information students need to
understand regarding their academic program pursuit. The Programme handbook outlines
Programme Educational Objectives, Programme Outcomes, the Curriculum with its respective
Course Learning Outcomes and course contents for courses across all levels.

6.4.

Standard of Tests and Examinations

The programme adheres to the minimum standards established by the NUC when designing tests
and examinations. Throughout the semester, assignments and assessments are conducted to
cover the course materials as much as possible. At the conclusion of each semester, individual
instructors or a team of lecturers responsible for a course develop examination questions for that
subject, integrating Bloom’s Taxonomy into the questions. These questions must thoroughly
address the course content. Sample examination questions along with marking schemes for all
levels of the Programme have been provided. Examination questions from 100 level to 400 level
are jointly reviewed by selected co-examiners, consisting of senior academic staff within the
Programme. The final review is conducted by the Head of Programme, who is the Chief
Examiner. For the 500 level, examination questions undergo external moderation by Senateapproved External Examiner(s). At the end of the session, the External Examiner visits the
Programme to moderate the assessments of graduating students, including their final year
projects. Examinations take place at the end of each semester.
The final grade for each student consists of the examination score with continuous assessment
marks. The continuous assessment includes tasks such as assignments, term papers, mini
projects, and laboratory works. These assessments contribute 40% to the total grade, while the

236

examination score accounts for 60%. The duration of each course examination varies based on the
credit value assigned to the course. For a 3-credit course, seven questions are provided, and
students must answer five within a three-hour period. Similarly, for a 2-credit course, students
are required to answer four out of six questions in two hours. In their final year, students
undertake projects worth six units. This project extends throughout the entire session as a
continuous effort, with final project defense occurring at the end of the second semester. During
this time, students defend of their project through an oral examination. In the final semester,
presenting seminars with PowerPoint and demonstrating their projects are mandatory
requirements. The seminar plays a key role in enhancing the intellectual growth and confidence
of the students.
(a) Admission to the Examination
Every student who is duly registered for courses in a given semester is qualified to take the
examinations in those courses, except in the following cases (subject to Senate approval based on
College Board recommendation):
(i) a student who stays away from the University for more than three weeks without
official approval.
(ii) a student whose attendance at lectures or practical sessions in any course falls below
80 percent.
Before being allowed into the examination hall, each student must:
(i) present a valid University Identity Card along with an Examination Card.
(ii) ensure that no unauthorized or incriminating item is in their possession or among the
materials they legitimately brought into the hall, such as rulers, mathematical tables, and
similar items.
(iii) maintain orderly conduct and strictly follow all instructions given by the Invigilator
or Examiner.
(iv) refrain from any form of malpractice or attempt to engage in examination misconduct.
(b) Examination Marks
The marks awarded in each examination are determined by the internal examiners (Lecturers)
and subsequently endorsed by the examination moderators and the Chief Examiner (the HOD).
For final year students, the involvement of external examiners is also required during the
moderation process. The final stage of moderation takes place at the meeting of the Programme
Board of Examiners. Where there is a disagreement, the recommendation of the external

237

examiners may only be set aside if overruled by a two-thirds majority vote of the Examiners
Board.
(c)

Board of Examiners

The Programme Board of Examiners is made up of all academic staff within the Programme, with
the Head of Programme serving as the Chief Examiner. The College Board of Examiners, on the
other hand, comprises all members of the College Board of Studies together with any other
individuals assigned the responsibility of examining courses offered within the College.
(d)

Semester Load

Each student is required to register in line with the Programme guidelines, carrying not less than
15 credit units and not more than 24 credit units per semester. However, students who have are
in special cases may, with the approval of the Vice Chancellor, be allowed to register for more
than the maximum credit requirement.
(e)

Pass Mark

The pass mark in any course of is 45%.
(f)

Grading System

The performance of a student in each course shall be presented in both numerical scores and
corresponding letter grades. The overall academic standing of a student for a semester is
expressed as the Grade Point Average (GPA). The GPA is calculated by multiplying the Grade
Point (GP) earned in each course by the respective course credit unit (C) to obtain the Weighted
Grade Point (WGP). The total WGP for all courses taken in the semester is then divided by the
total number of registered credit units to determine the semester GPA.
𝐺𝑃𝐴 =

The Cumulative Grade Point Average (CGPA) of a student is the overall average of the Weighted
Grade Points (WGP) accumulated across all semesters.
The procedure for computing the CGPA is as follows:
(a) Multiply the Grade Point (GP) obtained in each course by the corresponding course credit unit
C to determine the Weighted Grade Point (WGP).
(b) Add together all the WGP values for every course taken throughout the period of study.
(c) Divide the total WGP by the total credit units of all registered courses, whether passed or
failed, across all semesters.

Summary of the graduation requirements for the program

This section contains a summary of the process for ensuring and documenting that each graduate
completes all graduation requirements for the program
The curriculum for these degree programs is aligned with the approved COREN Benchmark
Minimum Academic Standard (BMAS). Comprehensive details are available in the program
handbook. To earn a Bachelor of Engineering (B.Eng.) degree in Computer Engineering at Afe
Babalola University, students must perform satisfactorily in Core/Compulsory courses,
Elective/Option courses, University requirements, Industrial Training, and adherence to National
Universities Commission (NUC) and COREN standards.
6.7.

External Examination System

The Programme appoints approved External Examiners to moderate final year examination
question papers and marking guides. The University regards the external examination system as
an essential mechanism for maintaining quality assurance and promoting the highest standards
of professionalism. The present External Examiners are:
